ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e114	Honourable co-representatives! Former poacher and vice-leader of the Socialist Left Otto Hauglin is dead, 70 years old. Hauglin was elected to the Socialist Electoral Union of Eastfold from 1973 to 1977. In the first two years he was a member of the Church and Education Committee and then of the Financial Committee. Hauglin was a engaged and reflected person, occupied by justice. In 1975 the Socialist Left Party was formed, and Otto Hauglin was the Vice-Chairman in the early years of the party, from 1977 to 1979. Then different policies were to be brought together, and Hauglin stood for a listening openness and a clear direction, without being divisive. He gave the game a width and openness that a newly started game on the left-hand side needed. Hauglin was safely located between a Christian view of life and a socialist view of human beings. He became controversial when he reserved on the issue of free abortion. But the party supported his right to his own position. Hauglin, however, was not a conservative. He was involved in liberalizing the abortion law and helped shape the law so that it placed greater emphasis on social conditions than before. The new section 55 A of the Working Environment Act forbade employers to ask for life views in case of new employment. Hauglin followed up as principal at the Diakon home in Oslo. He put down a ban on asking about life's views on employment. It cost him a lot of resistance from the most conservative Christian environments in Norway. Hauglin's strength and against the summed up well in the words he chose himself to tell how he got through the resistance: I wrote my sorrows in the sand and shattered joys of marble. After the Diakon home, he used his skills for various church bodies, educational institutions and the health sector. He found that much joy in it. The Greek island of Hydra became important to Hauglin and his wife, Magni. Many friends have been visiting them there. We remember Otto Hauglin for his work and shine peace over his memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e172	We are going to consider a proposal from representatives of the Progress Party asking the Government to provide regular reports on the state of energy in the state of the state of the state of the state of the state of the state of power, on the availability of different energy technologies, strategies for investing in the development of new energy sources and the status of international energy obligations. The majority of the Committee does not share the views of the proposers and considers that the proposal is not acceptable. I want to argue here for the majority's view and assume that the minority argues for it. Norway is today an energy storm power. It has not come about by itself, but is the result of active political choices. Through a long-term and targeted investment in both oil, gas and renewable energy, Norway has gained an impressive position in energy. The exploitation of our natural resources brings profits and ring-fences to the community. This is a misguided development, based on a comprehensive policy that sees the link between energy policy, industrial policy and the environment. The dialogue on energy policy is central in Norway. How we position ourselves in an energy sector changing are among the most important debates in Norway and a debate that is taking place here in the Chamber, in the Committee, in the fractions, in the parties and, not least, in the electorate. This government has put stones on energy policy. We are there today that much of the basis for the transformation we are about to undergo in the energy sector has been laid. We are now entering a period of large investments both in grids and in new power generation, and we have facilitated the powerful industry through, among other things. the CO2 compensation scheme. Proposalers want their own energy message for Stortinget to discuss energy policy. The majority do not share the view that this is necessary. The development of Norwegian energy policy has been presented in several documents to the Stortinget in recent years and has invited to such debate, among others. at the latest in the budget proposal of the Ministry of Oil and Energy for this year. Important parts of Norwegian energy policy have also been determined by the Stortinget treatment of Melding. St. 14 for 2011-2012 on the deployment of the mains power source, Notify. St. 21 for 2011–2012 Norwegian climate policy and Meld. St. 28 for 2010–2011 on the petroleum activity. For the last seven years, the government has laid the framework for Norwegian energy policy. The red-green majority has, among other things: ensured the public ownership of Norwegian hydropower, which is now threatened by the right parties, which wants to sell the legacy silver to private provided for a significant increase in power generation through the electricity licence market strengthened Enova and entered into a new agreement with Enova on the network development and upgrading of the power grid Energy policy is important. That is why it is also important that we debate energy policy in this Chamber. I understand that we are doing this, both through the energy policy issues presented to the Government’s Stortinget, and through representative proposals. I also allow myself to mention that it is not the first time in this ageing period that we are dealing with a proposal from the opposition on this very topic. I refer you to the consideration of the representative proposal 112 S for 2011-2012, document 8:147 S for 2009-2010 and document 8:24 for 2008-2009, which was all about the corresponding theme. The majority of the Committee does not agree with the proposers in this matter. The government's energy policy is set out in the Government Declarations and in the said Government Communications from this period. I refer you to the letter from the Council of State to the Committee where he writes: ‘The draft representative argues that one of the major challenges in the energy sector is the lack of a comprehensive, robust and long-term policy. The government does not share this assessment. According to the government's assessment, we are now on the right track and much has been done in energy policy. This also means that efforts in the years to come should focus on policy implementation.’ The majority of the Committee shares the government's view on the matter. Debate and discussion on energy policy are important, but I do not feel that a prerequisite for such a debate is a separate energy message. We already have a good, long-term debate on these issues. I consider with this setting as submitted.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e219	Norway is naturally blessed with large energy resources, and it makes us a significant energy nation. We have large resources, among other things. oil, gas, water, wind and bioenergy. By connecting our energy raw materials with knowledge and capital, Norway has become one of the world's leading energy nations. Energy resources have in turn contributed to high value creation, employment and prosperity. The Progress Party wants to strengthen Norway as an energy nation. That is why we have once again promoted the proposal that the government regularly promote a big message about the state of energy in the state of the state of the state of the state of the state. Unfortunately, but perhaps not very surprising, the government parties would not support us in this. Perhaps it has to do with the opinion of State Councillor Borten Moe that the ‘energy challenges of this nation’ are ‘solved’ from the Questionnaire on 14 November 2012. For us who do not agree that all the energy challenges in the nation are solved, but want to look forward, an energy message is an important piece. In order to make the best use of our energy resources, there is a need for a comprehensive and long-term energy policy. We believe that the red-green government has not delivered that. If we go back a few years, we see that the Council of State rejected the need for an energy message, while the Council of State of Åslaug Haga, on its side, announced a big message in 2007-2008. Then came State Councillor Terje Riis-Johansen from the Centerpartiet and put Hagas' performance project on the shelf in 2008. So Riis-Johansen turns in 2010 and announces that an energy message is still coming, and there was an energy selection put down to create a NOU. Today's State Council, Borten Moe, received the energy investigation in 2012, and the Ministry of Oil and Energy presents the status of energy policy, at the same time as the State budget, and endorses the energy investigation. It is obvious that the government is divided into energy policies, and it is the energy industry that loses, because of uncertainty, framework conditions and level of investment. It takes approx. 10 years from the initiation of a large energy project until its completion. It is then important that policy objectives, support schemes, governance and legislation are coordinated so as to avoid major delays as a result of an incoherent policy approach. Here the Progress Party believes that the red-greens have failed. The size has been little invited to focus on overall contexts and dimensions such as energy volume, time horizons for changes in consumption and production, costs and technological opportunities. The Progress Party considers that a comprehensive Landfill Communication on the State of Energy is necessary to ensure an ambitious but ground-based approach that can illustrate short-term and long-term power needs and production potential. It is not just the Progress Party that calls for an energy message. Key actors such as the NHO, LO, Energy Norway and several environmental protection organisations have also called for an energy notification. We think it is a pity that the government parties do not share this view. Overall plans showing the relationship between energy, environment, infrastructure, transport, housing and probably several other elements could give the government a plan to govern. Norwegian municipalities are required by Stortinget to make similar municipal plans and long-term budgets, but the government does not have to do so to Stortinget. It seems strange that today's government is refusing to develop an equivalent management tool. On questions to the last two oil and energy ministers, I have been told that there are enough bureaucratic plans, and the work on energy projects now needs to be started. Such developments are likely to facilitate the government on the same schedule as in the road sector, i.e. piecewise and divided.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e236	During this great period, we have debated on several occasions the need for an overall energy message. The Progress Party has also previously put forward proposals for a Landfill Communication on the State of Energy, and in the autumn of 2010 it presented a proposal for a representative, Document 8:26 S for 2010–2011 for a comprehensive energy policy, in which energy supply, climate change and industrial development are seen in the context. The Minister of Oil and Energy then expressed in a letter to the Committee of 8 December 2010 that a separate big message on energy policy was not relevant at that time, but we noted with satisfaction that the same Minister of Oil and Energy only five days later set up the so-called Energy Committee, which was to investigate precisely these issues we called for. The right was satisfied with this grip, and we looked forward to the government presenting an energy message for debate in Stortinget. This notification was also notified several times before these plans were parked in connection with the presentation of the 2013 State Budget, where the Energy Investigation was easily endorsed by the current Minister of Oil and Energy. This believes the Right was unfortunate. Many skilled professionals are behind the Energy Investigation, and this is a good document, which should have been the starting point for a more comprehensive treatment. Why is an own energy message important – or a big message about the state of the kingdom's energy? I will mention two main reasons: Norway is an energy nation. We have great interests, high value creation, many jobs and revenues to the public based on our energy resources. This is not an area that deserves to be treated piecewise and divided, but that needs long-term horizons and visions about how the industry will develop further. The energy field is strongly changing. Our closest neighbours in Europe have energy policies high on the agenda and are already discussing what their policies are going to be by 2050. In Norway, the Government Parties and the Minister for Oil and Energy are leaning back and content with their own policies, and boasting that they are able to look as far as 2020. 2020 is eight years ahead. Eight years are low in energy policy contexts. Energy policy requires long-term time horizons. The right is impatient to debate a comprehensive energy policy. We've been behind the introduction of electricity certificates, perhaps not so strange given that these were originally initiated by the Bondevik II government, but a comprehensive energy message could provide the basis for a better understanding of the market balances. In Norway and the Nordic countries, we are most likely to have a significant surplus of power towards 2020, including: as a result of the electricity licence market. How are we going to use this surplus? How do you dimension the exchange of power with the rest of the world in order to safeguard this situation in parallel with addressing the potential picture of closer integration with Europe? The right considers that in many ways the government is going backwards into the future by reducing the level of ambition of capacity exchange with the continent while at the same time sending a legislative amendment which will exclude private initiatives from improving this capacity. According to State Secretary Ane Hansdatter Kismul's recent speeches in the business community, the government is afraid that the revenue from private cables will not be able to fall to the community and subsidize the central network tariff. I advise the Minister of Oil and Energy to study the hearings that have come to mind in this matter, where, among other things, it is. refers to the fact that, through regulation, some of the profits can be returned to this type of use – regardless of ownership of the cables. In today's business, the group manager of Agder Energia also has an article that mentions the exact same thing. In his letter to the Committee, the Minister for Oil and Energy refers, inter alia, to: for the processing of the online message. Yes, all parties agree that we should strengthen the central network as mentioned in the notification, but once again it is only part of the issue being discussed. The notification does not refer to the underlying network levels, which are also ahead of large investments in the future. The notification did not contain any discussion on how to finance the costs of the large network investments in the future, i.e.: whether it is reasonable to base the current cost allocation or whether we should look at changes. Norwegian power-processing industries, such as Norwegian Forest, Elkem and Hydro, are already warning that they expect large increases in tariffs. The same will apply to households. Households are becoming more energy efficient. It is shown, among others: through a fairly stable level of consumption over many years, despite an increase in the number of inhabitants. How will the individual electricity customer react when the electricity consumption represents only a small proportion of the bill in relation to the network lease, from which the individual cannot make itself more efficient away in the same way? What is the government's response to the need for project management, prioritisation and cost control on the huge investments in the future? Yes, among others. the termination of Statnet User Council, which gave industry and customers an opportunity of transparency and influence. In addition, the answer is a lack of willingness to look at the structure of ownership of the many network carriers at the underlying voltage level.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e311	The representative proposal under discussion today argues that one of the major challenges in the energy sector is the lack of a comprehensive, robust and long-term policy. I do not share that assessment. On the contrary, I believe that we have now put in place the important framework conditions for the Norwegian energy sector in the years to come. Now is the time for the implementation and operationalisation of policies already adopted. A short time ago, the government presented an annex to the 2013 budget. The document provides a broad review of the government's energy policy, both in terms of what has been achieved so far, and in terms of the prospects for energy policy in the years to come. So I believe that the government has already presented its view of the state of energy. As set out in this Annex, a number of measures have been implemented by the government during that period. We have a strong escalation in Enova. Extensive action has been taken to improve the conditions of the mains; we will even deal with the concrete follow-up of legislation later today. There has been a significant increase in the effectiveness and effectiveness of concession treatment, which has contributed to a significant increase in the scope of concessions. Overall, government policies have helped to make the Norwegian power system less vulnerable to fluctuations in power generation, and regional power supply has also become less vulnerable. The government has also put in place a number of important measures to help improve the framework for further development in this field. Of course, one of the most important is the establishment of the Norwegian-Swedish certification market, which will ensure 26.4 new TWh with energy into the systems by the end of 2020. A policy has been drawn up through the notification of petroleum and the notification of climate change to facilitate power from land to oil and gas activities, and through the network notification, the government has defined the policy for the development and reinvestment of the central transmission system. An Action Plan for the Renewable Directive has also been presented, setting out how Norway will achieve the target of a renewable share of 67.5 pst. in 2020. I can therefore answer the representatives of Amundsen, Skumsvoll and Grimstad that the state of energy is good. That doesn't mean we have no energy challenges in the years to come. Significant investments both in the transmission system and in production shall be made in the following years. There are significant quantities of concessions still to be granted, both for individual projects in the water and small-scale, to wind, and not least for the transport of lines. It's going to take a lot of both the energy authorities and the energy industry. But the fact that the framework conditions have now been put in a good way, that the industry knows what to do in the years to come, and that healthy investment decisions have been made on the basis of that, is my clear view. The effort must now focus on the successful implementation of the energy policy to shape the energy system of the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e580	The amendments submitted to the 2012 State Budget, the use of powers during the petroleum activities and the participation of the EEAS in development projects on Norwegian continental shelf previously dealt with by the Stortinget are supported by the Committee without further comments. However, the part of the proposal dealing with energy efficiency improvement targets in buildings is more discussed by the Committee. The intent of this point is a follow-up to Report. St. 21 for 2011–2012 Norwegian climate policy, where the Stortinget asked the government to come back with a goal of energy efficiency improvement in construction during 2012. It was long unclear what is actually in this proposition in terms of energy efficiency improvement targets in barley. The Government refers to a target of 15 TWh energy efficiency improvement by 2020, but this turns out by more detailed questions to the Council of State and replies to the Committee solely to be an effect the government calculates from the already adopted policy, and that the very objective they consider to present for energy efficiency improvement in buildings is the following – I quote from the Council of State response letter to the Energy and Environment Committee dated 12 December 2012: ‘The government’s overarching and long-term objective is that new buildings should be resilient with a low environmental impact throughout their lifetime. Until 2040, the government will create a basis for thinking for building where materials, consumption of energy and water, the indoor environment, pollution, transport and waste are put into a holistic context. The government's ambition is that the use of energy in buildings per square metre should be significantly lower by 2040 than today.» I believe that the government parties in the debate will explain the majority's position on this matter. The right and the other opposition parties do not see this as a response to Stortingen's order, and I understand that the Council of State chose to present this so-called target in the form of a press release on Friday afternoon. No one can disagree with the ambition that the use of energy in buildings per square metre should be significantly lower by 2040 than today, and we have already adopted a policy that will contribute to this, among other things. through the last climate agreement in Stortinget in the spring of 2012, where we were asked for passive house level in 2015 and almost zero energy level in building from 2020. The right has long called for an energy efficiency improvement target in barley. The most environmentally friendly KWh is the one that is not used, and we have a potential for energy efficiency improvement in Norway. In addition, we are the only EU/EEA co-operation country that has not yet set an energy efficiency improvement target. The focus on energy efficiency has both an environmental and an economic dimension, but it also means something for the emergence of new technologies and smart solutions. By setting an ambitious target for energy efficiency improvement with related instruments, we could challenge Norwegian environments of competence and technology by creating a demanding domestic market for this type of product and solution. Energy efficiency improvement is also about industrial development, jobs and value creation. The government's goal or ambition is not good enough. The right and the other opposition batches want a clear, quantifiable and verifiable measure. We are therefore promoting proposals in line with this. I would also like to point out that the estimated impact of 15 TWh energy saving in construction towards 2020 as a result of already adopted policies also appears strange in view of the fact that trade environments such as the Arnstad Committee and the Low Energy Committee believe 10 TWh in energy efficiency improvement towards 2020 would be ambitious. Various environmental organisations have asked for the target to be set at 8 TWh – while the government therefore believes that we should achieve 15 TWh without any new instruments. Following questions from the Committee, the Council of State has explained how the allocation of these 15 TWh should be for the categories of private and commercial buildings, as well as for new and existing buildings. I must probably allow myself to point out that the expected impact on energy efficiency in existing buildings seems optimistic without new instruments. Among other things, it's only about 1 pst. by the mass of the building being replaced annually. This means that much needs to be taken into account within existing building mass, not least in households. We expected targeted action against this group, both because it represents a large potential, and because it does not have specific incentives within the existing instrument – despite the combined contribution of households and leisure homes to about 2.9 billion. kr to the Energy Fund, which is made available by Enova. A power of 15 TWh is also somewhat soluble as long as the government does not present a reference path against which this effect can be assessed. Therefore, it is proposed that such a reference path be submitted to the Stortinget during the spring of 2013. Unfortunately, I have to conclude that the government has not followed up the climate agreement on setting targets, and will finally address the proposals to which the Right is the co-rapporteur.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e648	In the climate agreement, we agreed that setting a target for energy efficiency in buildings was important. The majority then pointed out that a wide range of instruments, e.g. component requirements, technical regulations, Enova, etc., were relevant for achieving such a target. The majority therefore decided to ask the government to come back with a target for energy efficiency improvement in construction during 2012. The majority also pointed out that, in addition to the reinforcements in the building regulations, there was a significant potential for energy efficiency improvement in buildings, which uses – as has been said – 40 pst. of Norway’s net stationary energy. A proposal was also made for the Stortinget to submit proposals for instruments that could help trigger significant energy efficiency and energy conversion from fossil to environmentally friendly sources in private households. Thus, the climate agreement meant that the government would provide a figure for energy savings during 2012. That's what the government is doing in this case. Based on the measures adopted, a cut of 15 TWh is foreseen by 2020. It is significant and ambitious, but it is primarily an expression of the impact of measures already adopted. It takes time, as has also been said on the Right, to implement substantial on-site measures in the housing stock. In addition to this objective, the effectiveness of the component requirements adopted but not yet established will be achieved. It has also been decided to introduce passive house standard from 2015. Further efforts are being made to clarify what this standard implies, and that means that the effect of the passive house standard is also not included in the figure for 2020. It is important to say that the reference path for the calculations used in this case is the one that was established before the red-green government. The government has more ambitious objectives than that expressed in 15 TWh in 2020. The overall and long-term objective is that new buildings should be sustainable with a low environmental impact throughout their lifetime. The energy use per square metre in construction shall be significantly lower than today. Of course, I share that objective. After participating in various discussions and meeting during the Doha climate summit, I saw that energy savings in many countries have a more central place in the discussion than in Norway. This is due to the fact that the main measure to reduce fossil energy use in many countries where heating and electricity production are carried out with fossil energy sources is to reduce the use of energy in buildings. This is not the case in Norway, where the main energy use in housing is renewable, but our ambition is to save this energy in order to use it to replace fossil energy in other sectors, such as energy. transport. The climate agreement points to the need for instruments both to achieve energy savings and to facilitate the transition from fossil fuel oil. Some of these instruments are already available in Enova, in the House Bank, in the form of energy requirements for products, etc. The latter is to be further worked on. It must also be done, according to the climate agreement, in following up the fact that we are constantly under enough pressure in the energy conversion and that the means we have are giving the most efficient energy savings benefit.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e667	After Iceland, Norway is the country in the world that uses the most electricity per capita, and we stand out clearly negative in terms of energy use in construction. So we have a huge untapped potential for energy efficiency improvement. As the representative Rudihagen said, the cheapest and most environmentally friendly energy is the one we don't use. Therefore, the ambition to achieve energy efficiency must be as high as possible in all. The energy use in construction today is approx. 44 TWh in dwellings and approx. 36 TWh in professional construction. The target is 2040, and the target is so concrete that ‘the energy use in construction per square metre shall be significantly lower in 2040 than today’. It is not a target. That it “should be significantly lower in 2040” – almost 30 years ahead – than it is today, yes, it is a matter of course. Yes, a lot has to be done on the road, but it is not a measurable measure. Another reason why we have talked about 2020 is that the EU has had a major focus on national targets for cut and energy efficiency improvement by 2020. What's interesting when you look at the calculation of the 15 TWh to save is that it's figured out -- I'll quote from the proposition, page 9: ‘In order to find energy efficiency gains, energy use in building is first calculated in 2020 as if all newly built and rehabilitations followed TEK 97. Next, the estimated energy use in 2020 has been given that all buildings will be built according to the new regulations. The difference between these two calculations represents the energy efficiency improvement.’ It's a very interesting way to figure out a measure. Now it's time for New Year's Eve, and it's like I'm going to say that next year I'm going to lose 15 kg, and then I'm going to count on the 15 kg I slimmed down last year. The starting point would have been quite different if I hadn't done what I did last year. It's a very special way of thinking about a measure. It shows that the government has no energy efficiency improvement target at all. A calculation of the effects of instruments to be provided or adopted. It's okay, but it's disappointing, because I wish we set an ambitious goal. I might be correcting myself a little bit because I've seen that the government has had relatively many goals, whether it's been about cleaning Mongstad, about Kårstad, about 3 TWh wind power, about getting an energy message, or what it might be. There have been many objectives that one has not managed to achieve, and therefore it is clear that the instruments are the most important ones. ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e693	Energy efficiency has been high in Norway. For households, however, there has been a slight increase in energy use since 1992 and stable energy use in recent years. That doesn't mean that an increment doesn't work, because there are some other factors that also affect energy use. The population has increased by 20 pst. Since 1990, people live on an increasing number of square metres, and increased wealth makes many higher demands for inner comfort. Energy efficiency improvement is effective: energy use without insulation, heat pumps and savings bulbs would be at least 20 pst. higher than today. But it is still the case that the instruments that have been put in so far do not take us to the goal. We need to reduce energy use, not just stop growth. This will require more stringent measures, such as the 2015 passive house standard requirement, the energy presentation requirements for building materials and components, and a much heavier effort by Enova towards energy efficiency improvement. Previous speakers pointed out and illustrated the living problems with the objectives of the government's proposal, and he also pointed out the lack of tools to do something that is really effective in raising energy efficiency. The left will introduce a tax exemption for environmental investment in owner-occupiers to effectively trigger the potential for energy efficiency improvement in the existing housing stock in Norway, and we will cultivate Enova as a tool for major projects and expansion in industry and municipalities and counties, as well as more stringent technical standards for new buildings. ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e709	On behalf of the government, I have presented targets for energy efficiency improvement in barley. It is a follow-up and a receipt of the Storting decision in the context of the treatment of the climate notification, where the Stortinget asked the government to come back with a goal of energy efficiency improvement in construction during 2012. The overall, long-term goal of the government is that new buildings should be sustainable, with a low environmental impact throughout their lifetime. By 2040, the government will start thinking of building where materials, consumption of energy and water, the indoor environment, pollution, transport and waste are seen in an overall context. The government's ambition, the measure, is that the energy use in construction per square metre shall be significantly lower in 2040 than it is today. Through this long-term objective, the government is signaling a clear expectation to the industry that the 2040 constructions will be much more than just energy-efficient. We are promoting a holistic approach to the sustainability of construction in the broad sense. By doing so, we believe that it will be essential for Norwegian construction to adapt to the low-emission society. They should also be healthy to live in; it should be a good indoor climate. So it's not like we're starting from a zero point. As the representative Rudihagen was involved in his post, we already have the strictest demands on a wide range of building components, we have among the strictest requirements for technical standards on construction in the world, and for private households in its part, we have already taken significant costs related to technical regulations, but also as such. There are many decisions and measures taken over a long period of time which mean that Norway is already largely exploiting its energy pretty effectively today. The industry itself must be involved and take responsibility for identifying future materials, construction methods and transport solutions. In the Climate Communication, we signaled that we would increase the energy requirements of the building technical regulation to passive house level in 2015 and to almost zero energy level in 2020. It's incredibly ambitious. A decision on the level of requirements is made in the light of studies on the socio-economic and health consequences and the competence of the construction industry. The Government will therefore subsequently lay down the provisions defining the different levels. This government has a comprehensive instrument aimed at improving energy efficiency in buildings. So let's do that through the Enova, we'll do that through the House Bank. Calculations show that it will deliver an effect of 15 TWh energy efficiency in construction by 2020. We believe that this is ambitious but realistic. I would like to stress that an effect of 15 TWh energy efficiency in construction does not necessarily mean that energy use will decrease accordingly. There are many factors that affect the development of energy use, and they are moving in different directions. The main drivers of increased energy use are demographic and economic developments. Energy efficiency in construction moves towards delimiting energy use. I point out that the government has other instruments, such as taxes, such as information and advice, that help trigger energy efficiency measures in buildings. The type of energy efficiency measures is also affected by other factors, such as prices, changes in construction practices or technological developments. On behalf of the government, the NVE has calculated that the effect of minor maintenance and refurbishment work in existing buildings will be 3 TWh between 2010 and 2020. Of these, dwellings account for about 2 TWh — in other words, a not insignificant amount of energy.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e892	Today we are dealing with the setting of changes to the Energy Act, and that is an important matter. We're in a period of big investment in the energy industry. In this situation, it is essential that we have a good and effective process that looks at the development of production and networks in the context. The changes presented in Prop. 41 L for 2012–2013, comes as a follow-up to the Storting decision during the processing of the online notification earlier this year. Ensuring energy supply and access to energy facilitate the development of wealth and industrial development are among our key tasks as politicians. Electricity transmission through transmission lines is often a controversial issue of major local and national interest. It is important that we ensure a concession process that is professionally strong, transparent and trusting. I believe that the changes that are now being made to the Energy Act facilitate a more flexible and better procedure. Under the new regime, the Department will be involved earlier in the process, which makes political responsibility clear. The decision-making competence of large network projects is transferred to the King of State Council and opens up for external quality assurance of design choices. These are important and correct measures that will help to streamline processes and reduce the level of conflict. Secure and predictable supply of electricity is crucial for society. Electricity is currently a prerequisite for most of the processes in households, public and business. The Committee also considers that the development of the power grid is a priority task and that the concession treatment period and the effectiveness of the concession treatment process for network projects should be strengthened. I would like to thank the colleagues in the Committee on Energy and the Environment for the speed with which we have been able to deal with this matter. It is a unanimous committee that supports this proposal. With this I recommend setting.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e909	This proposition is a follow-up to Report. By the changes we are adopting today, we want to achieve a prior political involvement in the major power lines and a more effective concession process. An important element of this new solution is the possibility of requiring an external quality assurance and the ministry issuing an opinion to the applicant’s assessment of needs and design choices. For the right, it is important that quality assurance is trusted by different interest groups, and it is therefore essential that the professional quality is high and that no one questions the independence of the actor who actually carries out the analyses. ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e926	I would first like to thank the Stortinget for the prompt treatment of the present bill. The amendments to the Energy Act are a follow-up to the network message notifying changes to the concession treatment for major power lines. I am pleased that a unanimous committee supports the new rules proposed by the government. A strong and robust electricity network is important in order to meet the growing need for stable and good power supply in society. Therefore, Statnett is now undertaking significant investments in the network. The development of large power lines is decisions on how many are affected and conflicting considerations need to be weighed and weighed. I have great respect for the fact that it will always be affected that is not satisfied with the decision that is finally taken. For this reason, thorough and good procedural processes are particularly important. At the same time, it has been shown that the procedure for large power lines projects takes too long. It is therefore necessary to take action to improve the effectiveness of the procedure, without prejudice to the quality of the procedure itself. The legislative proposal provides the legal basis for imposing external quality assurance on general questions of principle and introduces a prior political involvement. The aim is to strengthen the governance of energy authorities with the concept choice. We do that by ensuring the professional quality of the decision-making basis. The government believes it is important to set the framework for the electricity pipeline project early, so that it becomes clear which alternatives are involved. Thus, further consideration of the concession issue can be focused and reduced to more detailed questions about the remaining, remaining, alternatives. The changes in the concession treatment of major power lines also imply that the decision-making competence is transferred to the King of State Council. Major power lines are of major political and social importance and therefore decisions should be taken at the highest level of administrative law, following extensive and very thorough concessional treatment both in the NVE and in the ministry. At the same time, the Government proposes to coordinate the decision-making competence for power lines which are dealt with together with a case under watercourse law where the King of State Councils takes decisions. This will result in a more efficient and transparent process. In order to implement the changes signaled in the network message, regulatory changes are also required and a regulatory guide is to be established to clarify the legislation. The Government aims to implement the necessary changes in the near future. We are now in a very active period for the development of the transmission network in Norway. It is necessary to ensure the best possible power supply for all of us. At the same time, we need to make it possible for all renewable energy – whether from water or wind – to be fed into the network. With the overall regulatory framework we are now getting in place, we should be well aware of an efficient and administratively very good concession treatment of power plants in the years to come.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e1056	Today, we are making an important proposal to delineate the State's right of reciprocity in municipal planning and land-use matters. This is an important theme and, at a number of previous times, the right has proposals to strengthen municipal self-government in planning and land-use matters. Two years ago, we had a comprehensive proposal to strengthen local democracy and property rights in environmental and land management, including: making changes to planning and building laws. Control from above or from below is a simple word in the political debate that shows the difference between red-green policy and what the opposition stands for. Right wants growth from below, and it characterizes our whole political basic attitude. We believe that the spread of power makes society stronger, and that strengthening local self-government and much more respect for private ownership are an important foundation for such a spread of power. The fact that the individual is to have a great deal of power over his own life situation has been central to Norwegian culture for all years. The municipalities themselves form the foundation wall of the local government, and the municipalities represent both the connection and the possibility to influence the future of the inhabitants. That's why the Right wants more power for the municipalities. One of the biggest harms after seven years of red-green rule is the harsh participation of local democracy and private ownership from the three government parties. Despite the finer words of increasing local self-government, the right of access to large areas and values is transferred from local authorities to state authorities. This process has led to a crisis of confidence between local communities and central authorities in many places. Moving power challenges a tradition where natural areas have been managed through largely gentle local use for generations. Increasingly, a decision relating to protection and land use is perceived as a conflict between local and central authorities. Despite large words from the government party's side, practical policies have shown that both local democracy and private ownership have had far worse conditions than that of former bourgeois governments. I have previously mentioned a good example of government overmanagement from my own home state, Sogn and Fjordane, where a private developer wanted to build three cabins at Endestadwatten. The planning and development selection in the municipality of Flora has, in several respects, been reconciled with the supported regulation plan, and the municipality adopted the plan on 16 June 2009. Due to correspondence from County of Sogn and Fjordane and comments from the Directorate of Nature Management, the regulatory plan was sent to the Ministry of Environment, where he was rejected in Decision No 29 June 2011. On the right, local authorities in the municipality of Flora and other municipalities will decide on such matters. Greater local jurisdiction will lead to greater legitimacy for the decisions that will be taken. It will facilitate greater local participation; not least, bureaucratic duplication of efforts can be rationalised. Local self-determination guarantees closeness to decisions, with the right of citizens and a living local democracy. Municipalities should therefore play a more central role in local social development. For the Right, growth from below is more important than control from above, and therefore, the Right will strengthen the local self-government. The right of state bodies to object to municipal decisions in planning matters, as we see in this case from the municipality of Flora, is the main reason why municipal self-determination is restricted. Unfortunately, local politicians' judgements are rarely taken into account in such cases. Right is the fact that there must be some form of State counterpartition, which ensures that national sovereignty is guaranteed, and that the municipalities comply with the laws and regulations of accumulation and government. We therefore do not want to abolish it completely, as this proposal puts it. But the Right is concerned that the counterclaim or the threat of contradiction, which, according to Right's opinion, should be the last resort, has unfortunately almost become the main rule in local land issues. Therefore, the Right cannot support the proposal as it stands, but suggests that it be added to the meeting book. In the opinion of the Right, therefore, there is a need for a reform that would limit the rights of State authorities and restore power to local politicians. Therefore, the right-hand side would like to see it. to limit the possibility for County State to review municipal decisions only for the purpose of law enforcement and complaint management, so as to eliminate the possibility for County State to review municipalities based on judgement. Decisions based on judgement, where different considerations are to be emphasised, are at the very heart of democracy. Therefore, the Right believes that one should leave this to elected local politicians, not government officials. And then I'll deal with the proposal that the right-hand position has.

As many have experienced, one has even offered financial assistance for the implementation of clean-up measures. Yet little or nothing is done. As many people have experienced, it is almost difficult to understand – when one sees the enormous consequences it has for nature in the areas around – that the Russians are not willing to do more themselves. The emissions of Norilsk Nickel contaminate groundwater, rivers and lakes, as well as the emissions being carried by air. As suggested by the proposal, the proportion of respiratory diseases in the Russian regions is much higher than elsewhere and life expectancy is lower. It has also – according to Russian official figures – found more disease in children, more spontaneous abortions and premature births. In Norway, Clif provides its own dietary council for the consumption of reindeer meat in the Norwegian border area due to heavy metals. I would also like to pay tribute to the Minister for the Environment for the very thorough presentation in the letter of reply to the Committee. I agree with the Council of State that there is no alternative to continuing the dialogue in this matter. The Joint Declaration of the meeting with President Medvedev in Norway in April 2010 provides a good starting point for further work. It is established here that emissions from nickel production at the border are of concern and must be reduced to a level that does not harm health and the environment in the border area. From the Russian side, helping to put in place the necessary measures to reduce emissions – that is good. At the same time, it is not uplifting to see that Russian authorities show so little willingness to put pressure on the owners of the nickel plant to implement clean-up measures. Still renewed exemptions for emission permits and inability to impose requirements on owners for faster emission reductions give rise to concern. Of course, one would also wish that the Russians had given more attention to the cross-border aspect, that emissions also create problems in Norway. I therefore agree with the Council of State that we should probably have some expectations of the opportunities to achieve rapid reductions in the emissions of Petchengankel. However, I believe that the government should continue to address the matter with the Russian authorities through political channels with renewed strength. The Joint Declaration of 2010 should be used to remind Russia that emissions need to be dealt with rapidly. In addition, we hope, as the Council of State also stressed, that we will be helped by Russia joining the OECD and by Russia joining international agreements and conventions. The aim must be to find a solution to the nickel issue, which serves the health and environment of the border area, which promotes good neighbourliness, and which helps to create the best possible cooperation in the north. I support the Committee's position that the proposal should be annexed to the protocol.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e1708	The problem with the cross-border pollution from nickel production on the Russian side of the border is and has been a particularly difficult matter. For the government it is a high priority case to find a solution that takes care of health and environment in the border area. The representative's proposal relates to the same issue as the interview with the representative Jan-Henrik Fredriksen, whom I responded to in Stortinget on 17 April this year. In my post I would therefore like to carefully update the status of the case and an assessment of what we should do. Since President Medvedev's visit to Norway in 2010, we have been working hard to follow up the joint declaration of that meeting, where one at the highest level in Russia has recognised that emissions from nickel production in the border areas are a problem, and that Russian governance should help to put in place the necessary measures to reduce emissions to a level that does not harm health and the environment. As part of this follow-up, a meeting of Norwegian and Russian government powers and experts was held in July this year. The company was also well represented. It was quite clear here that although both sides think that they are facing an environmental problem that needs to be addressed, there are different perceptions of how serious the problem is. On the Russian side is one of the most busy emissions of sulphur dioxide, while on the Norwegian side our professionals think that heavy metal is the biggest problem, which gets less attention in Russia. There was a consensus that environmental monitoring in the border area should be strengthened and that information to the local population on the actual environmental situation should be improved. We're now working on following up. Russian government tells us that no one can demand faster reductions than the enterprise's tolerances. It's a cross of thought for me, all the time the company has had a huge surplus in recent years. From the company's side, it has been stated that they aim to satisfy the requirements of the ordinary emission path during 2015, which they do not do today. This is likely to result in emissions of about 70 000 tonnes of sulphur dioxide per year. In view of that, I think we should have a close relationship with the possibilities to achieve rapid reductions in emissions. However, there are some points which suggest that there may be a basis for cautious optimism in this matter: One is the modernisation of the Russian economy, which forces itself because there is a alarming need for the replacement of inefficient and out-of-date production equipment. It also largely applies to the plant in Nikel. The second is the revision of Russian environmental legislation that is in progress and could provide Russia with an environmental law of international standards. The third is that Russia wants to become a member of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development, the OECD. A membership here would mean that Russia would have to accept the basic principle as an environmental liability of the OECD. Fourthly, efforts are being made to get Russia to accede to relevant international conventions and agreements in the field of the environment. The signing of such agreements will give Russia the same obligations as Western countries, which will make greater demands for action, including in this matter. I believe that the only dangerous way forward in this matter is an active dialogue with Russian government powers and the group as well as strengthened environmental monitoring. Of course, we have no possibility of putting power behind the demand for reduced emissions, and I don't think we're going to set this case up against other cooperation between Norway and Russia. Norway will continue to address its concerns about the release of Petjenganikel in a clear way to Russian government powers. It will include: shall take place in the Norwegian-Russian Commission for Economic Cooperation, which shall hold its next meeting in February 2013. I have also written a letter to the Russian Minister for Natural Resources and the Environment, Sergey Donskoj, and asked for a meeting with him. We would like to have the best possible dialogue with Russian authorities and share our experience in finding good solutions. However, I would like to stress that it is the Norilian Nickel group and the Russian government that are responsible for emissions coming down to a level that does not harm health and the environment in the border area.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e2146	Alnabru freight terminal is hub in Norwegian freight transport. The terminal is at the centre of gravity of freight transport in the Oslo area, and Alnabru is a crossroads for all lines in the same area. Goods are loaded from track to track and from car to track from car. Alnabru is more than just a terminal. With the financial crisis, we had a natural decline in the number of goods transported on the road. We saw the same decline in the proportion of goods transported by other means of transport. The difference is that while the proportion of goods by road and sea has recovered slightly afterwards, the proportion of goods by road is still low. At the same time, it is said that the demand for freight capacity on trains is today greater than the offer. The NHO report, entitled Quality assurance of the concept investigation for the renewal and extension of the Alnabru freight terminal, concludes that a treble in the carriage of goods by road is unrealistic and that the railway system has over-dimensioned the Alnabru project. It's going the wrong way. The proportion of goods on the track goes down, while the number of trailers on our roads just increases. This development has been going on for many years, despite the government's expressed objectives of something quite different. It is a political goal – and it is cross-political – to transfer more goods from road to rail. But to get this done, the industry needs political drag help. The Alnabru terminal can collapse over the next six to seven years, according to the Railway Agency, i.e. the government's own body. The collapse of Alnabru can provide 300,000 more car combinations on our roads per year, with reference also to NHO Transport. Today's technical plant at Alnabru is old and worn. In any case, if the terminal is not rebuilt, there will be a need for extensive renewal of the installation within 10-20 years – without necessarily increasing capacity for that reason. The use of the Alnabru terminal is in the process of reaching the ceiling, as well as the preparation of the railway technical facilities for replacement. They're old, and something has to be done. A quality report ordered by the Ministry of Transport to look at the Alnabru Plan of the Railway Agency states that the development should take place more quickly than previously foreseen by the Railway Agency. At the same time, this report also states that development can take place at a much lower price. The railway system itself has developed a good strategy aimed at doubling the volume of freight carried by train by 2020 and tripled by 2040. One freight train replaces between 25 and 30 wagon trains. A doubling of the capacity of Alnabru will therefore remove a total of 2000 trailers from Norwegian roads each day. This corresponds to a reduction in annual emissions of 78000 tonnes of CO2 and 520 tonnes of NOx. Such road-to-road freight transport also involves up to 20 fewer fatalities or injuries to road traffic annually in a road safety context. This means, on the one hand, savings in socio-economic costs of between 300 million. NOK 350 and SEK 350 million. kr each year, and on the other hand – not to forget the most important thing – this will also represent the absence of suffering affecting individuals and their families. The growth in traffic to which the Railway Agency’s good strategy implies a need for approx. 11 km with stowage tracks on Alnabru after the first construction step. Today, 4 of 17 rail tracks on Alnabru are reserved for CargoNet, the public part, and 4 for the private Cargolink. At the same time, CargoNet controls the transhipment and machinery park at most terminals, as well as the workshop that also repairs the trains. If the activity is measured proportionally, we will also see that the activity is greater on the 4 private lines than the other 13. In comparison – to get a picture of it – this means that the runways on the Gardermo should have been owned by SAS, a small strip of Norwegian, and all the other companies that wanted to land on these strips had to ask about law and, of course, pay to land. Then everyone realizes that this is not a good solution in society when we transfer the same logic to Alnabru. The 17 tracks on Alnabru should have been managed as a common resource, preferably — possibly — by a modernised railway system that could ensure a level playing field, flexibility and not least competition on these tracks. In the right we think that we see little involvement from the government in this area, which in terms of road safety, industry and the private labour market, has a very important impact on Norway's competitiveness. The railway works overestimate, I think, the amount of goods that will be transported by railway in the future. A tripling of freight traffic is not realistic. Since the railway works have planned a tripling, they have also reached a need for upgrading that amounts to approximately 10 billion. NOK, plus 3 billion. NOK for the purchase of land and land, i.e. land. If the cost is to be so high, it is clear that this is also coming into competition and in priority with other types of investment that we sorely need in the transport sector. Until now, the Railway Agency has looked at the possibility to triple the capacity of Alnabru. But with the planning of the police's new emergency base at the same location, the Railway Agency also has to consider other alternatives and look at the exploitation needed at Alnabru. I've been up there traveling the area, I've talked to the transporters and I've met the various actors in the area, and we believe that 30 measures of the site must be used for police preparedness, as it looks today and with the information we have. At the same time, we don't know because the details of what this will mean for the area are not sufficiently known. From the right, we find it very worrying that the government cannot appear as fully aware, and that we do not get an overall assessment of the whole area, so that all the facts are clarified in this context when two different businesses are going to live side by side. As it now appears, this seems a little bit co-ordinated and small-scale. There is now a quality assurance of the development plans for, inter alia: providing sufficient security to continue the correct deployment options and implementation strategy for the development of the freight terminal. In addition, consideration shall also be given to the ability of the police helicopter base to fit into the area. But independent of the emergency centre – this is not an obstacle to building step 1 on the Alnabru terminal, as we see it – there is a desperate need for someone to start the renewal process. What I'm asking is, and the right question is, why doesn't the government start right away? Director Enger of the Railway Agency has even recommended that this be expanded as public-private cooperation, also with public-private funding. The right considers that the Minister of Transport should listen to the subject matter and initiate the construction of Stage 1 as an OPS project already in 2013. It is important that the uncertainty surrounding the emergency centre does not lead to further training of construction steps 1. It is important to get this work started immediately, both because the existing terminal has reached the capacity limit and because it will remove about 2000 train trains from Norwegian roads every day. It costs a lot. The investment needs of Alnabru are high, but a diet/benefit analysis also shows that development will be among Norway’s most profitable transport projects in modern times.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e2203	Initially, it is important to establish two things: the government's goal of transferring more goods from road to sea and track where it is socially beneficial has not changed. Secondly, the development plans for Alnabru have not been scrapped. I am concerned that we are implementing an extension to Alnabru that provides sufficient capacity and that the renewal of the facilities is taking place in a timely manner. The situation of goods on the road is an important theme. I will therefore try to explain what the government has done, and what it will do in the future. I want to put the emphasis on Alnabru, since the representative Schou in his interpellation focused just on this terminal. The Alnabru terminal is, as the representative was, for freight transport what Oslo S is for passenger transport. The terminal itself is the hub of rail freight. The Alnabru Terminal has enough capacity to receive some more goods than is currently being handled, but in the longer term the capacity of the terminal needs to be expanded. In addition, the technical facilities at Alnabru are old and worn out. Words like collapsing become very tabloid. However, it is clear that the Railway Agency estimates that the renewal of the technical installations will have to start before 2019. If we postpone this renewal, operational stability will go down. The cost of repairs and maintenance will increase. With lower operational stability, rail competitiveness in freight will also be reduced. A well-functioning hub is a prerequisite for achieving the goal that we have in common, of more goods on the railway. In the current National Transport Plan 2010–2019, a gross cost estimate of 1 billion was made. kr to start of the Alnabru project. After several years of planning, the railway works have developed a master plan for a first of four construction steps at Alnabru. The Master Plan was in place in 2010 and the cost has been estimated at more than 10 billion. kr. In view of the significant increase in the cost allocation that existed, the Ministry of Transport saw the need for a review of the existing development plans and launched an external quality assurance of the development plans in December 2011. This quality assurance work was completed for the first six months of this year. The quality assurance carried out showed that the railway works master plan for stage 1 would in itself provide a good and functional freight terminal. However, the quality assurance raised several fundamental questions on the basis of which the railway works were planned. In particular, the expected capacity needs and the appropriateness of maximising the capacity of Alnabru were involved. The quality assurance showed that the Railway Agency has made plans for a terminal that is much larger than is likely to be needed. Overall, construction steps 1 to 4 of the railway works master plan will approximately triple the capacity of Alnabru. Construction stage 1 will give approximately a doubling of the capacity on the terminal. The quality assurance carried out in the summer points to the fact that the future demand for the terminal will be about twice today, i.e. it corresponds approximately to construction stage 1 of the master plan. So, of course, the question is, why can't you start working on the planned construction step 1 right away. The railway station’s planning work at Alnabru has resulted in a premise that the terminal should be developed in such a way as to triple the capacity in the long term compared to today. Therefore, construction stage 1 was designed for a further extension of the terminal at a later stage. Alternatives that did not provide this possibility were found early in the Railway Agency’s investigation of various development options at Alnabru. In fact, the premise of a possibility of subsequent tripling may have resulted in more socio-economically profitable solutions to double capacity being insufficiently assessed and analysed. The Ministry of Transport believes that it is important to consider in more detail whether the solution options could have been designed differently and more reasonably if another objective had been set as the primary from the outset. With construction stage 1, the expansion of the terminal will take place in a single step, while in the years to come the capacity requirement will be more gradual. We have to live with the fact that it is not realistic with a linear capacity extension, and a smaller stage of deployment split is likely to reduce the risk of overinvestment in capacity at a later stage. As regards the establishment of the police emergency centre, the quality assurance showed that it will be possible to establish this type of emergency centre on Alnabru while at the same time realizing the first construction steps in the master plan – i.e. a doubling of the capacity of the terminal. However, it will not be possible to extend the terminal beyond the first stage of construction in such a way that at a later stage a triple as indicated in the original master plan will be achieved. In summary, it is important to say that there are a number of factors that have now commissioned the Ministry of Transport to look at alternatives to the planned development. Expected capacity needs appear to be too high and have placed strong limits on what alternatives are considered possible and realistic. These conditions render it no longer relevant to triple the capacity of Alnabru. In other words, there have been changes in the assumptions after the work on the development plans was completed by the Railway Agency. Once the main premises for the investigation work have changed, it will only be wrong to close your eyes and say: Go on! The expectation related to the development of freight volumes in the medium and long term indicates, according to the Ministry of Transport, that it would be sensible to facilitate approximately a doubling of rail freight capacity. There should be an overall ambition for the development of the Alnabru terminal. The existing building stage 1 plans at Alnabru increase capacity from the current level of 600 000 TEU to about 1.1 million in one jump, while the development of demand is expected to take place more gradually. The Ministry of Transport has therefore commissioned the Railway Agency to propose solutions to Alnabru which involve a more gradual extension of the terminal than the existing development plans provide. It shall also be considered whether it is possible to obtain more reasonable deployment options in view of the fact that the terminal is land-limited to approximately a doubling of capacity. I want a development plan where the terminal develops as much as possible in line with demand, rather than expanding everything at once. With this kind of approach, the aim is to speed up the development process in order to be able to increase the capacity of Alnabru early in the coming national transport plan period. Step-by-step developments would also be appropriate if the market were to develop more positively than the projections currently forecast. I am concerned that the work on further developing the Alnabru terminal can get started quickly. In its mission to the Railway Agency, the Ministry of Transport has therefore specifically asked for an assessment of the possibilities for action during the first four years of the next national transport plan, including: renewal needs and expected capacity needs in the light of current Alnabru residual capacity. This may be a first step in the longer-term modernisation and renewal of the terminal. The first phase of the investigation shall be at a conceptual level, i.e.: an overall assessment of possible solutions. The Ministry of Transport aims at ensuring the quality of the investigation externally in the autumn of 2013, with a view to further detailing the selected development and renewal option from 2014. The knowledge we now have, for example: as a result of the external quality assurance initiated, there is a need to ask questions about the existing Alnabru development plans. The massive increase in the cost allocation from the current NTP to the master plan is one element that has reinforced that need. In addition, in recent years, rail has received strong competition from cheap foreign road transport. It is therefore important for measures that can reduce the costs of freight carriers on the road. A more efficient and modern terminal is the type of action. There has been great impatience in the further development of the Alnabru terminal. I share this impatience. We can't wait ten years for a further expansion of Alnabru. With a step-by-step and more realistic approach than the original approach, it is my view that the capacity of Alnabru could be increased much faster than originally planned. In addition, the government will return to more concrete actions in the national transport plan for the period 2014-2023, known to the Assembly, to be presented to the Stortinge in the spring session next year.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e2423	This is a matter of Norwegian approval of the EU Council Directive on common rules in the field of civil aviation. Civil aviation is an activity where it is very appropriate that rules exist between the countries affected by a flight. Already in 1947, the Chicago Convention on Civil Aviation was signed by 52 countries. It was the start of the so-called International Civil Aviation Organization, abbreviated ICAO, established in 1947, and Norway ratified this agreement on 5 May 1947. In addition to describing how ICAO is to be organised, the Chicago Convention facilitated the harmonisation of legislation and procedures to promote safety and simplify and streamline cross-border aviation. In Europe, a body of common rules on aviation safety, the European Aviation Safety Agency, was established in 2002, abbreviated by EASA. The purpose of EASA is to ensure a high and uniform level of aviation safety in Europe. EASA shall also contribute to a level playing field and financial savings for the aviation industry. The Agency has a total of 31 members: the 27 EU countries and Norway, Iceland, Switzerland and Liechtenstein. Norway became a member of this on 1 June 2005 in connection with the inclusion in the EEA Agreement of an EU legislation containing common basic aviation safety provisions. This legislation also established EASA. Basic legislation under the responsibility of EASA is adopted by the European Parliament and the Council of Ministers, while technical implementing legislation is adopted by the European Commission after EASA has drawn up proposals. What we are about to decide today is an extension of the EASA area of competence through a Council Directive and the decision of the EEA Committee related thereto. This applies, inter alia, to: the certification of personnel and organisations involved in flight operations, the flight operations themselves and third-country operators. In addition, it is open that the EFTA Surveillance Authority, ESA, may, at the request of EASA, impose an infringement fee and periodic penalty payment when the holder of a certificate issued by EASA does not comply with the regulatory conditions of this certificate. The main objective is therefore to ensure a high level of safety in civil aviation through harmonised legislation in Europe. The Regulation makes it necessary to amend the Aviation Act in order to empower ESA to impose economic reactions if necessary. There is an overall committee which here submits its recommendation of consent to the approval of Decision No EEA Committee. 163/2011 of 19 December 2011. I therefore recommend the Committee's position.
