ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e2469	The Council of Europe Anti-corruption Committee, GRECO, has assessed the Norwegian part-financing and made a number of amendments. This has now been followed up by the government and proposed changes based on the recommendations. However, the government has also proposed other changes that do not result from the GRECO recommendations. The Committee has been consulted on many of the amendments, while there is disagreement in other areas. An overall opposition is united on a number of proposals, and in addition, the different opposition parties have their own proposals in addition. I expect the government parties and the opposition to present their own proposals. However, an overall committee is committed to ensuring that the legislation on the financing of the parties should contribute to good democratic processes and to transparency. At the same time, it is important that the law on voluntary work in the service of democracy does not become more bureaucratic than necessary. The Committee points out that voluntary work in small lot associations is part of forms and reporting obligations to scare many away from trust, and that this may in turn lead to poorer recruitment to local political work. The Committee recalls that the basis for the present Party Law was laid by the Committee on Subsidiary Financing, NOU 2004: 25. The selection power at most essential points to provide an overall setting. The proposition focuses on the law of the parties and on the funding of the political parties, the use of money and the specific use of money in the election campaign. The Committee notes that GRECO has a specific focus on voters’ demands for transparency about the electoral budgets of the parties, but will at the same time point out that equal budgets are used by large organisations both within and outside the labour market to influence voters’ votes. The Committee would point out that it is an international phenomenon that large funds are channelled outside the parties in the election campaign and that there is a need for more transparency in this respect also in Norway. The members of the Committee of the Progressive Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party ask the Government to set up a selection to ascertain whether transparency considerations are sufficiently protected by legislation relating to campaigns and money use from sources other than the political parties. The Committee notes that the Ministry of Proposition proposes that the batch organisations also provide expenditure accounts. The Committee notes that GRECO is opposed to the conclusion of the Lot Funding Committee, which considered that the grounds for introducing such reporting were weaker than the arguments for not doing so. The Committee notes that the main justification given by the GRECO, which the Ministry submits, is that the voters should be able to see how the tax money is used by the batches. The Committee agrees with the proposal for the introduction of an obligation to provide expenditure accounts, but would stress that this should not compromise the autonomy of the lots to apply the batch aid freely, without the State having to monitor or impose conditions on the use of the aid. On a general basis, the opposition is skeptical of public control over what democratic political parties spend their resources. Supervision can be seen as a potential democratic problem. The Government proposes, in violation of the Committee on Budgets, in particular the reporting of electoral campaign contributions above NOK 10 000. In the opinion of the opposition, instead of specific rules for election campaigns, it would be better to have a continuous publication of reportable contributions, at least every four weeks. The batches should be subject to continuous flagging of contributions above the thresholds, at least at a central level. Contributions received on Friday before the election day will not be published before the election day due to delays in bank systems. The opposition also points to the fact that several batches already publish contributions on their websites, on the basis of such a self-imposed deadline, and considers that it will comply with GRECO’s recommendation if such self-reporting is made mandatory for all batches. This follow-up to the GRECO recommendations to Norway is of course important. But at the same time, I ask a lot of questions about whether the parties are going to present absolutely everything they do now, for public access. While it is clear that contributions made to the political parties should be made in public, we are questioning whether it is necessary to have own rules for election campaigns with their own limits on the amount of contributions to be made before they are published. On that basis, we think it is unfortunate that there is so much political disagreement about this. The party law should be something that all the parties stood together about. I am disappointed that the government has not made any more effort to have contact with the political parties before this matter was brought forward. I am addressing those proposals where the Right is the co-rapporteur.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e2736	Development is primarily created by sound national policies. International relations are important, but the choice of countries to meet globalisation and the desire of their own citizens for a better everyday life is crucial. Political institutions and processes ensuring the distribution of power, the rule of law, democracy and human rights are central. In addition, openness comes to the outside world, which stimulates trade and private investment. In the right, greater emphasis will be placed on the organisation of local authorities for their own economic development. An open economy with stable framework conditions conducive to local and foreign investment, contributing to increased trade, is a prerequisite to generate tax revenues for public services and to grow out of poverty. The world as a whole has had an amazing prosperity development. First of all, it's because of it. economic growth that 450 million people have been working out of poverty in the last 15 years. It's happened especially in countries like China, India and Vietnam. In the last 50 years, the average income in the world has tripled. The common denominator is a more open economy and extensive international trade. Trade is the strongest driver of economic growth and thus of the ability to create jobs and economic prosperity. There are still too many poor, but at the same time there is good reason to rejoice that the proportion of poor is at its lowest ever. Already in 1994, researchers at Princeton University published a research report showing a positive correlation between wealth levels and environmental protection. This context has since been repeated in a number of publications. For the right, this is very important. On the one hand, we need economic growth in order for new millions of people to be able to work out of poverty, but at the same time we depend on greenhouse gas emissions and environmental damage to be drastically reduced. Another key development is that both the number of democracies and the proportion of the population living in democracies is increasing. According to Freedom House, three-quarters of all countries are free or partially free. The number of democracies has doubled in the last 30 years. There are no democracies without a market economy. Transparency and freedom of choice are fundamental rights to give people power and influence. Free trade and property rights are also key components. Closed countries are only able to provide economic growth and well-being for their own population to a very limited extent. Oxfam calculated that if Africa increased its share of world exports by only one percent, it would generate increased revenues of about 70 billion. dollar. In 2006, the World Bank estimated that foreign workers sent home 250 billion. dollar. Only because of tax evasion is it estimated that developing countries lose 160 billion. dollars a year. In addition, there is an illegal flow of capital. The right-wing objectives of development policy are to contribute to increased democratisation, to the realization of human rights and to the sustainable working out of poverty. The right will never accept the cross-cutting nature of development policy. It is important to emphasise coherence in development policy, i.e.: to help all Norwegian policies towards poor countries move in the same direction, whether in terms of trade policy, aid, education policy or health policy. All countries, including poor countries, have the primary responsibility to enforce political, civil, economic, social and cultural rights for their inhabitants. The right also believes that Norwegian development policy should play a role with local policy authorities where possible, and encourage local ownership of priorities and projects financed by Norwegian funds. Such a desire builds upon, and builds upon, democracies, and we will demand that we also accept decisions that we do not necessarily agree with. But developments that go across universal human rights, the Right will always respond strongly to them. It is economic growth that has been the strongest factor for, and the contributor to, the vast majority of people who have worked out of poverty in the last 15 years. Therefore, it is a primary objective of the Right that our development policy should contribute to economic growth. Economic differences between countries generally decline because emerging economies are growing faster than the most developed economies. Here's the big inequality. At the same time, differences are increasing internally in a number of countries, both in terms of income and access to basic welfare services. Discriminating and poor population groups in most regions do not participate in economic growth. Of course, growth needs to be distributed in order to honour the rights of the poorest. There are a number of reasons for poor distribution beyond lack of or poor distribution policies. Discrimination and non-regulation are the two most important explanations in many countries. It is important to highlight the link between distribution and growth growth. For the Right, it is important that each cooperating country should have greater ownership of its own economic development. Right-wing wants to support the efforts of the co-operative countries to strengthen their education and health systems so that the poor have access to welfare services. Strengthening the tax system will also be an important instrument. In this way, we can contribute to fulfilling the rights of poor and marginalised populations. The development of the tax system and the distribution of growth are up to each country, but we can contribute with our competence. The majority of the world's poor are in middle-income countries. It is primarily a distribution challenge, not a development problem. Democratisation, equal rights to education and enhanced civil society are more appropriate instruments to address this problem, as well as national distribution policies. Zero growth makes it impossible to combat poverty – the main threat to life. But growth at the cost of the environment makes it impossible to preserve the most important source of life – our natural heritage. Right will pursue a coherent development policy aimed at increasing democratisation, respect for human rights and helping people to work long-term out of poverty. The objectives must also lead Norwegian trade policy. Norwegian self-interests must subside where they conflict with these objectives. In parallel with significant aid efforts, Norway has maintained high customs barriers and defended a trade policy that creates effective barriers to the export opportunities of developing countries. It is striking to see the contradictions in Norwegian policy. The OECD has estimated that agricultural aid in developed countries amounts to 300 billion. dollar per year, nearly three times as much as all official assistance. Improving the opportunities for developing countries to participate in the global economy is a prerequisite for people to work long-term out of poverty. Trade and economic growth are the strongest driving forces. Corruption is one of the strongest obstacles to economic development. Corruption undermines the rule of law and democracy, leads to decisions that are contrary to the interests of the community and deterrent to investors. Unfortunately, it is the poorest who are hit hardest by corruption. They cannot afford to pay bribes to safeguard their rights and must bear the main burden of the impact of corruption on the economy, employment and the environment. The fight against corruption thus becomes a crucial part of the fight against poverty and a very important tool for giving individuals more power over their own lives. In the right, greater emphasis will be placed on the organisation of economic development by local authorities. An open economy with stable framework conditions that promotes local and foreign investment and increases trade is a prerequisite for this. The concrete measures to be taken to ensure good governance will necessarily vary from country to country, as different countries face different challenges. However, for the Right, the objectives of parliamentary control, control of civil society authorities, capacity building and anti-corruption will underpin development policy regardless of national variation. To make poverty response more effective, more targeted efforts are needed especially towards vulnerable groups. It is crucial to address ever-increasing differences, including in middle-income countries. Girls and women, especially those living in rural areas, belong to many vulnerable groups. 70 pst. of the world's poor are girls and women. In its 2011 annual report, the World Bank showed that discrimination against women slows down economic growth and that gender equality is one of the most effective instruments in the fight against poverty. Furthermore, the World Bank says that more political participation by women leads to better public services, such as water supply, schooling and less corruption. The UN Secretary-General has argued that gender inequality is slowing development within all the eight thousand-year targets.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e2782	I would like to start by saying a thousand thanks to Peter Skovholt Gitmark for drawing up this debate. We're going to deliver a big message in the New Year about just fair distribution, democracy and growth, and taking an initial debate and hearing what the opposition is bringing to square is interesting to us. I think that's a good opportunity for us, and also a good opportunity for the opposition to make recordings for the big shot. The overall objective of Norwegian development policy is poverty reduction and fair distribution. The reason is that fighting for societies with little differences is fighting for communities where there is more trust between people. Trust between people is essential for having good societies where people can live good lives, and it is also crucial for economic growth. American Economic Review vol. 100 concluded that trust between people in a society is the single most important factor in creating growth in a society — not spectacular. These are values that we also set high in Norwegian society. Sustainable economic growth is a prerequisite for poverty reduction, especially in the poorest countries. But growth alone is not a sufficient prerequisite. To get good societies, to get good development in developing countries, the inequalities between people need to be reduced. Too many inequalities are not only negative for society, but it inhibits economic growth and keeps more in poverty than a fairer and wiser policy would do. This is the experience we have gained in Norway and the Nordic countries, and it is supported by more recent development research. As is known, several developing countries have experienced high economic growth over a number of years. This has contributed significantly to the decline of extreme poverty in the world. The World Bank believes that UN Millennium Goal No. 1 – that the proportion of lye poor in 2015 is halved compared to 1990 – was already reached in 2010. Those who are lukewarm – a bit of that word – live for less than $1.25 a day, i.e. for $7-8, and the World Bank estimates that in 2008 there were 620 million fewer people living below this poverty line than in 1990. The number of low-income countries was also halved between 2000 and 2011, from 66 to 32 countries, and there is a positive trend. But the World Bank's calculations also show that there has been a great deal more that is only slightly above the ‘lut poverty limit’ of $1.25 a day to live for, which means that a large growth has led to a limited standard of living for the poorest, and the danger of falling back into ‘lut poverty’ is far too many. In addition, the greatest poverty reduction has taken place in Asia, and especially in China. Africa in particular is lagging behind – not least due to high population growth and unfair distribution. So the vast majority of the poorest now live in middle-income countries. I believe that it speaks for us to focus much more on inland distribution than we have done before to reach the poorest. If we just pursue a development policy towards the poorest countries, we will refrain from doing anything for 70 pst. by the world's poorest, and at least that's something that our government isn't prepared to do. This is also important because more recent research under the auspices of the World Bank shows that high economic growth itself does not lead to better distribution within countries. In other words, the proportion of poor people in a country does not necessarily decrease even if the country experiences high growth and the number of poor people falls. Moreover, the higher the economic disparities in a country initially, the higher the growth needs to be in order to reduce the proportion of the poor. World Bank analyses show that economic growth is leading to the greatest poverty reduction in countries with a high degree of economic equality. On the other hand, growth in countries with large differences has little or no effect on the proportion of poor. The researchers conclude that the ability of growth to reduce poverty depends on inequality. In other words, if you are concerned about combating poverty, you need to be concerned with a fair distribution policy in those countries where you want to achieve growth. Two examples can illustrate this: Indonesia had negative economic growth at the end of the 1990s, but managed to reduce the proportion of the poor through active redistribution policies. In other words, growth is not necessary to eliminate the poor, but redistribution policies are necessary to achieve it. In Peru, on the other hand, the proportion of poor people in defiance rose to almost 4 pst. average economic growth in the decade since 1997. In other words, growth does not necessarily lead to a fair distribution and elimination of poverty. This shows how important a fair and wise distribution policy is. One of the things that we've at least been kneeling very clearly is that inequality is necessary to free up capital for investment, is completely wrong. The Nobel Prize winner Joseph Stiglitz said it very clearly in the first BBC program that was about abolishing poverty. He said very clearly that the belief that only you have economic growth, you get a trickle down effect, that is, that the growth spreads down to everyone, now something we recognize is just nonsense! Important reasons why high levels of inequality can hamper economic growth are that it can contribute to the reduction of cohesion and trust in society, to conflict, and to the weakening of institutions, democratic accountability, transparency and criticism. We also know that large differences in access to education and health weaken growth capacity, because it creates inequality in opportunities. Effective poverty reduction in developing countries requires policies that promote growth – with jobs and fair distribution. As the Asian Bank says, growth and equality can be seen as parts of a good circle: greater equality in society facilitates higher growth. An active redistribution policy has three beneficial effects: it reduces poverty in the short term; it facilitates the more effective reduction of poverty in future growth; and it improves opportunities for long-term growth. A doctoral thesis at the University of Oslo in 2010 found that economic growth has historically been higher in democracies than in authoritarianly driven countries. You can find one dictator who's busy with his people, but you rarely find three pieces in a row. This is particularly important for middle-income countries, which have more to distribute, and where most poor people live. The average income here is higher than in the poorest countries, but it is very unevenly distributed. Research suggests that the scale of redistribution in the same country does not need to be large in order to eradicate extreme poverty in the country — in most countries less than 1pst has been suggested. of gross domestic product. In other words, it does not take much to eliminate poverty; it is about political will in the countries we are talking about. In addition, I believe that developing countries in the intermediate income group should consider more active redistribution measures, for everyone needs to see their own policies in relation to the country itself. Norway’s efforts must be two-fold: in some countries where we have a long history of cooperation, we can actively engage in direct dialogue with the authorities. Here, we can also use the assistance strategically, targeting concrete actions that will improve distribution. However, it will also be important to contribute to greater international attention in the area of distribution policy. In this way, it will be important to strengthen the UN Organisation and the World Bank’s ability to promote good strategies. Last week I met the World Bank's new leader and said clearly that from Norway's point of view it is to ensure that the World Bank's programmes in poor countries are structured with a clear distribution policy, to have a clear distribution profile, is crucial for our contributions. I believe that the relationship between fair distribution, democracy and growth will be more and more central in the international development debate. The shift in power in the world due to the progress of new major powers has, in my view, led to a too unilateral emphasis on economic growth in the fight against poverty. I want Norway to be a spearhead, not only for sustainable and green growth, but also for a fairer distribution and for democracy in developing countries. It is not only morally and ethically correct; it is, in fact, the best thing to promote both poverty reduction and economic growth.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e2937	I would also like to thank the interpellant for raising a topical and important question. I want to start a little at that end when using Norway as an example of the wealth and distribution policies that exist in Norway. If you go back a little in history — to about 1800 — Norway was a poor country, the vast majority of them living on the poverty line. Why? You had a little elite who had made rules, which meant that ordinary people couldn't trade. Ordinary people were excluded from the economic growth that was in the country. You had a little elite sitting on top and earning a search warrant. What did you do in the 1800s? Yes, these rules have been removed. They were removed step by step and nutrition was introduced. It was said that everyone would be allowed to trade. You didn't need a citizen's letter and you lived in a place of purchase. The contractors were redeemed. The principle of nutrition was established. What happened during the 1800s? Indeed, economic growth gradually increased. With the industrialisation, the entrepreneurs came on the track and started new businesses and factories. What happened? Prosperity spread to the broad community of the people, both in the cities and in the countryside. In this way, the broad population was removed from poverty. If someone at that time had come to say that a Norwegian model is to be introduced, where you have to have a large public sector, where you have to have a high tax level and all those things, you would probably choke the positive economic growth that was the foundation for this. Then we agree that we must have a policy of distribution. The Progress Party has every year criticised aid that goes to countries where a dictator lives in sus and sobs, while the population otherwise lives on the poverty line. There is nothing new that has come now. We've been saying that all along. We have also said that economic development must be at the bottom. There must be one pie to divide. It must be large enough to lift the entire population. The problem today is that many of the poorest countries have so little gross domestic product, such a small production of income relative to the population, that if they had all been distributed, they would still have had great poverty in the country. In order to overcome poverty in the poorest countries, you need to get the economic engines up and go. You have to get productivity up, so you have a bigger pie to distribute to others. It is then important to see the importance of entrepreneurs creating the new jobs. Eva Kristin Hansen was in the workplace – I fully agree with her. Workplaces – giving people an independent income – are probably the best way to get people out of poverty. But if you're going to do that, you also have to have those who create jobs and make room for entrepreneurs, give room for ordinary people to start their small businesses, start their businesses, so that they can build productivity and have a tax base that can contribute to distribution. If you don't start at the right end, I'm afraid you're choking the economic growth that you're really looking to build up. I think that if you want to introduce the Norwegian model in many of these countries, you can achieve two things. One thing that can be achieved is that these countries get a public sector that is too large in relation to what they have the capacity to bear. What happens then? Either they become even more dependent on assistance from the rich part of the world to get the budget to go around, or they end up in a debt problem, which makes you become addicted. What we want is for these countries to have an economic development that will enable them to maintain their dignity, independence and independence and build their own engine. The system to be used for distribution is one that needs to be developed in each country. You can't come from the outside and say that things have to be distributed like this. There must be internal mechanisms developed in each country that ensure a good distribution profile in each country. I think it is also necessary to build the political institutions and build the legal security system to ensure good development. Thinking that the Norwegian model, with a large public sector and a high tax rate — at worst a socialist social model — is the solution for the poor countries, I think is a siding. I think that can lead many of these countries into a worse situation. What these countries need least is an experiment with Norwegian socialist neo-imperialistism to be launched in these countries.

As far as development tax is concerned, it is exactly the same. If, through our programmes, we contribute to increasing the tax revenue of a country, while they choose to raise the money in a few hands, are we going to spend Norwegian aid money on that country? And finally, if we spend money on education, while we see that the country itself refuses to increase its tax revenues to help just mobilise national resources to take over the responsibility for this education, is it right that we should do that?

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e3305	Now we're dealing with the 2012 budget that's just out of date. It is not a dispute over the budget either for the current calendar year or for the next one. It is an update of the budget, with technical adjustments and new estimates. The update shows that the budget has been strengthened. The Norwegian economy is strong. The estimate for oil money use has been reduced compared with the national budget presented in October. We can enjoy that. The reason is that both the expenditure estimate is reduced and the revenue estimate is increased. The estimate of taxes and charges from the mainland economy is overall unchanged from the national budget. When expenditure has been lower than estimated in the budget last autumn, it is primarily due to lower expenditure on sick leave and other schemes under public health. In line with usual practice, we do not use reduced expenditure or increased revenue to increase investment in other areas in the slack of the budget year. We would also not cut the budget if the picture were the opposite. We are challenged as to why we follow the rule of action so strictly, when the estimated use of oil money in any case changes by several billions throughout the financial year. But it is when the budget is presented that we can measure whether we succeed in complying with the rule of action, in making the right use of oil money, in giving a proper budgetary impulse. This is when the budget is presented – not when the phase is presented – we can measure whether we are able to contribute to predictability and safe economic governance. But are the estimates good enough from the beginning? To that I would say the following: the development of tax revenue depends on the development of a number of uncertain sizes, such as employment, wage growth, construction, car purchase, private consumption and business profitability. Changes also tend to move in the same direction, for example: Employment growth could be combined with higher wage growth. In a budget of over 1 000 billion. Even small percentage changes can mean several billion kroner. Thus, the fact that budget figures change as well as developments are known through the financial year is not, in itself, alarming. One way to measure the precision of estimates is to compare with other countries. The figures show that the revisions in the Norwegian tax estimates are not particularly large in an international perspective. For 2012, the changes are also clearly smaller than we have seen in the last few years. We must also bear in mind that developments in the budgets in the years following the financial crisis have reflected the uncertainty about macroeconomic estimates. There are still uncertainties and imbalances in the world economy. The real crisis in Europe is the unemployment crisis. Millions of people face an uncertain future. Norwegian export companies also notice the crisis in several ways. The experience of the last ten years shows that our fiscal framework is working well. Norway has done a lot right. But we've also been lucky. If the oil price falls sharply, the gear that pulls the Norwegian economy forward can go in reverse. During the financial crisis, several countries felt that what they thought was stable tax revenue failed when the economic cycle turned upside down. Similar events can also happen to us. Now we have good times for the Norwegian economy. In good times, we guarantee the basis for the future.

At the same time, new measures have been put in place for Greece in the last round, and the framework for a euro area banking union has also been put in place, which, in my view, could help build confidence in the euro and what is happening in Europe. But the fundamental challenges in the European economy have not been solved, with deficits, high debt burden and not least an increase in unemployment over the year. 25 million people enter the new year without a job, many of them with little hope for the future – young people who have been unemployed for a long time, who lose the opportunity for good education and to get a foothold in the workplace. In other words, there are major challenges in the area around us, and there is reason to be aware of what is happening with our closest partners in Europe. But all of these are themes that we'll come back to at different crossroads in the year we go into. As a speech to the representative Tende: I am very much in favour of an open and conscientious debate in many areas, and I hope that we can continue to have good debates on economic issues and challenges in 2013. Finally, let me just say the following, and that's that it's going well in the Norwegian economy, but there's challenges that we're going to get back to. I also want to say that there is no coincidence that we are where we are. It's a result of hard work over a long period of time, in which many people have taken part. It is not natural that this will continue, but our most important defence agency is that we can jointly build greater resilience into the Norwegian economy. With those words we can take a little bit of time off, but at the same time be observant of what meets us around the corner when we go into a new year in a little bit. There are enough challenges to address in the Norwegian economy, and not least there are enough of the challenges in the countries we see around us. But by holding a good and tough course, we have greater assumptions than many others, and it's important that we use that opportunity together.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-12-18.ana.ud1543e3487	The government budget is settled in balance before borrowing transactions, as a responsible person has to do. Gross financing needs are therefore determined by the difference between lending and repayment of appropriations over Cape. 5999, State resources, heading 90, loans. Gross financing needs are estimated to be around 124.7 B.C.E. kr in 2013. For the treatment of Inster. 5 S for 2012–2013 the Stortinget adopted an increase in relation to the government proposal under Cape. 2410, State loan fund for education, heading 90, increased loans and interest payable. This has consequences for Cape. 5999, heading 90. In the event of an incursion, this was not taken into account when the Stortinge decided to grant Cape. 5999, heading 90. The Financial Committee therefore recommends that the Decision of 14 December 2012 be adopted under Cape. 5999, heading 90, is cancelled and that Cape. 5999, entry 90, is granted NOK 124 722 009 000 for 2013.
